<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TbMXWvApKj8JNDGTbvLLVn`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	A growing body of work augments language models with external tools \citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}.
5	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches LLMs to insert API calls via self-supervised loss filtering; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask; ReTool \cite{retool-2025} applies RL to discover when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.
6	These focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}
7	Recent concurrent work sharpens this question: \citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect in LLM tool use, \citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax'' where tools degrade performance, and \citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity--utility--affordability framework.
8	Our counterfactual decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, $90.4\%$ of routing signal at $P{=}100$ traces to catastrophic-failure prevention.
9	
10	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
11	CogRouter \cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing across heterogeneous models.
12	RouteLLM \citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.
13	These optimize \emph{how} to route; we reveal \emph{why} routing helps---in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.
14	
15	\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}
16	Prior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.
17	Our approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \emph{before} any router is trained, sidestepping compounding perturbation artifacts.
18	
19	\paragraph{Safe RL and Catastrophic Failure.}
20	\citet{garcia2015comprehensive} survey safe RL methods that constrain policies to avoid catastrophic outcomes; \citet{crpo-2021} develop principled constrained optimization with formal convergence guarantees.
21	Our counterfactual decomposition reveals that death avoidance is the dominant component of routing signal, suggesting that modality routing functions primarily as a safety mechanism rather than a generic capability-matching problem.
22	
23	\paragraph{LLM Agents in Interactive Environments.}
24	ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment.
25	ReAct \cite{react-2023} interleaves reasoning with actions and serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection.
26	Recent agent architectures augment planning with world models \citep{itp-2026, dynathink-2025}, which inherit hallucination risks.
27	Our deterministic modality provides exact state transitions, and our analysis shows this exactness matters almost exclusively for death avoidance.
28	
```

</details>

          " ",
          " \\section*{Limitations}",
          " ",
          "-Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.",
          "+Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.",
          " ",
          " \\section*{Ethics Statement}",
          " "
        ]
      }

[Director 指令] [Patch] R8-W3 — Capacity scaling episode-level eval 结果

Appendix G (Capacity Scaling) 需要更新，纳入 episode-level 数据。**注意 Reviewer 审查后的保守 framing**：

**数据**：
| Model | Step bal_acc | Episode bal_acc (4 seeds) |
|-------|-------------|--------------------------|
| 0.6B | 52.8% | 50.67% ±2.62% (≈ random, CI crosses 50%) |
| 1.7B | 54.1% | 47.10% ±2.20% (< random, different training config†) |
| 4B | 53.5% | **58.96% ±1.94%** (significantly > random) |

**Reviewer 要求的 framing（严格遵守）**：
1. 不能说"性能随容量单调增长"——0.6B 不显著，1.7B 训练配置不同是混淆变量
2. 正确表述："4B first breaks the random baseline at episode level (58.96%, 95% CI [55.1%, 62.8%]), suggesting that model capacity is one limiting factor for episode-level generalization"
3. 必须加脚注：†1.7B episode-level figure (47.1%) comes from the main experiment (§4.3) with a different training configuration; direct comparison across scales should be interpreted with this caveat
4. Step-level 三个模型基本 flat (52.8%-54.1%)——说明 step-level 不受容量影响，episode-level 才是容量敏感的

**对 C2 叙事的修正**：
- 之前："structural limitation — capacity scaling 不改善"
- 现在："at the 1.7B scale used in our main experiments, episode-level generalization remains at chance; the 4B result suggests capacity is a contributing factor, but even 4B achieves only 59% — far from a practically useful router"
- 核心 claim 不变：death avoidance 的 task-type level binary nature 仍然是主要解释，只是不再说 capacity "完全无效"

同步检查：§4.3 (Router Training) 和 Conclusion 中如有 "capacity scaling shows no improvement" 类措辞需要相应软化。

编译后确认页数。这次不用验证引用。

```text
282	\section{Ablation Studies}
283	\label{app:ablations}
284	
285	\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B. Step-level balanced accuracy shows no consistent improvement: 0.6B achieves $52.8\%$, 1.7B achieves $54.1\%$, and 4B achieves $53.5\%$. This confirms the router failure is structural rather than capacity-limited.
286	
287	\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\% vs.\ 42.5\%), confirming that reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.
288	
289	\section{Cross-Environment Analysis: ALFWorld}
```

This confirms the router failure is structural rather than capacity-limited.\n\n\\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \\leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\\% vs.\\ 42.5\\%), confirming that reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.\n\n\\section{Cross-Environment Analysis: ALFWorld}",

```text
42	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic}
43	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
44	
45	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. A classification head on LLM hidden states degenerates to always-deterministic across all configurations. An implicit router (Qwen3-1.7B fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) yields $54.1 \pm 5.4\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes (8 seeds)---below always-deterministic (50\%). This is structural, not data-limited: $32{\times}$ data scaling, 0.6B--4B capacity scaling, majority-vote baseline, and per-task-type evaluation all confirm no learnable routing signal beyond always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Deployed end-to-end (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), the router ($-10.70$) does not outperform always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). We verify label quality in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
46	
47	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, with a larger differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\ref{app:failure-modes}--\ref{app:ablations}.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01YEBraJnbecrPzsdA4AxtyJ`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We introduced episode-level counterfactual analysis for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes (11 of 30 task types, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry. The DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$) at $P{=}100$, with strong cross-task correlation ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$).
4	
5	Cross-environment evidence supports this finding: on MATH ($N{=}300$), the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable without death penalty ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$). A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, 70 episodes) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks with a larger differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$). An exploratory router trained on counterfactual labels achieves only $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes---below always-deterministic---a structural limitation confirmed by data and capacity scaling ablations.
6	
7	In environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety: the routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection rather than efficiency optimization. Open directions include step-level counterfactual labeling, stronger backbones with lower intrinsic death rates, and environments with graded failure costs.
8	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011MsXpjLfKhWeR4rk2frh1Z`</summary>

219	
220	Decomposition metrics are stable under bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples of the 245-episode dataset): DAF $= 90.6\% \pm 4.2\%$ (95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$); Pearson $r = 0.975 \pm 0.011$ (95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$). The death avoidance dominance finding is robust to both resampling and split perturbation.
221	
222	\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. Of the 11 task types, 7 have \emph{all-deterministic} labels (no internal-preferred episodes exist), making balanced accuracy undefined---the router has nothing to learn beyond the trivial always-deterministic default. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\% to 73.0\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. This complements the data scaling and capacity scaling ablations: routing signal does not transfer across task types because the signal \emph{is} the task type---whether a given task carries death risk.
223	
```

37	
38	    \item \textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}
39	    On ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry. The penalty-dependent DAF metric reaches $90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ but varies substantially across penalty settings. An exploratory router trained on these labels fails to outperform the trivial always-deterministic baseline on held-out episodes ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ vs.\ $50.0\%$); data scaling and capacity scaling ablations confirm this is structural---when DAF $= 90.4\%$, always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
40	
41	    \item \textbf{Cross-context validation.}

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\\ref{tab:capacity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Step Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) & Ep.\\ Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-0.6B & 52.8 & $50.67 \\pm 2.62$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & 54.1 & $47.10 \\pm 2.20$ \\\\\nQwen3-4B & 53.5 & $\\mathbf{58.96 \\pm 1.94}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-det & 50.0 & 50.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy. Step-level accuracy is flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\\%$). At episode level, 4B first breaks the random baseline ($58.96\\%$, 95\\% CI $[55.1\\%, 62.8\\%]$). $\\dagger$\\,1.7B episode-level figure comes from the main experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) with a different training configuration (8 cross-split seeds vs.\\ 4 seeds for 0.6B/4B); direct comparison across scales should be interpreted with this caveat.}\n\\label{tab:capacity}\n\\end{table}\n\nStep-level balanced accuracy is essentially flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\\%$), indicating that step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, however, the 4B model first breaks the random baseline ($58.96 \\pm 1.94\\%$, 95\\% CI $[55.1\\%, 62.8\\%]$), suggesting that model capacity is one limiting factor for episode-level generalization. Nevertheless, even 4B achieves only ${\\sim}59\\%$---far from a practically useful router---and the dominant explanation remains that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently."
}

          " Decomposition metrics are stable under bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples of the 245-episode dataset): DAF $= 90.6\\% \\pm 4.2\\%$ (95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$); Pearson $r = 0.975 \\pm 0.011$ (95\\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$). The death avoidance dominance finding is robust to both resampling and split perturbation.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. Of the 11 task types, 7 have \\emph{all-deterministic} labels (no internal-preferred episodes exist), making balanced accuracy undefined---the router has nothing to learn beyond the trivial always-deterministic default. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\\% to 73.0\\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. ```text
42	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic}
43	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
44	
45	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. A classification head on LLM hidden states degenerates to always-deterministic across all configurations. An implicit router (Qwen3-1.7B fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) yields $54.1 \pm 5.4\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes (8 seeds)---below always-deterministic (50\%). At the 1.7B scale used in our main experiments, this limitation is structural: $32{\times}$ data scaling, majority-vote baseline, and per-task-type evaluation confirm episode-level performance at or below chance (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Scaling to 4B yields the first episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), but remains far from practically useful. Deployed end-to-end (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), the router ($-10.70$) does not outperform always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). We verify label quality in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
46	
47	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, with a larger differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\ref{app:failure-modes}--\ref{app:ablations}.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011DRaq8CNMEg4bwt2dYzrfs`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}
5	Does routing signal persist without catastrophic failure? On MATH Level~4--5 (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, internal reasoning directionally outperforms: 81.0\% vs.\ 76.7\% accuracy ($+4.3$~pp, 95\% CI $[0.0, +8.7]$; McNemar $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$). The label distribution inverts: 30 internal-preferred vs.\ 17 deterministic-preferred (cf.\ 9:86 in ScienceWorld), with 84.3\% no-preference. In the absence of death risk, the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable---consistent with death avoidance as the primary driver. A complementary evaluation on ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) confirms that the counterfactual framework identifies different routing mechanisms across environments: the deterministic advantage there stems from information asymmetry (access to admissible actions) rather than death avoidance (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Penalty sensitivity.}
8	The $r = 0.97$ correlation is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$); defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$). The correlation emerges only at $P \geq 50$ ($r = 0.86$) and reflects that non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Death is effectively the \emph{only} component generating differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. These findings may be capability-dependent: the 42.9\% internal death rate reflects a 7B model's hallucination vulnerability; larger models with lower death rates would reduce DAF, potentially revealing non-death routing signal currently masked by catastrophic failures.
9	
10	\paragraph{Implications for router design.}
11	The dominance of death avoidance (DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$) reframes modality routing as primarily a \emph{safety} problem rather than an efficiency-quality tradeoff. The routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection---closer to a conservative default policy than to a learned safety critic---aligning more closely with safe RL~\citep{garcia2015comprehensive} than with cost-quality optimization~\citep{cogrouter-2026, ares-2026}. The extreme label imbalance (86:9 deterministic-preferred, 150 no-preference) is not an artifact but a central result: among episodes where modality matters, deterministic is preferred at 9.6:1, explaining why always-deterministic is a strong baseline and why the trained router (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) adds no value. A lightweight death predictor may capture most routing benefit without full counterfactual labeling. In principle, routing could be fully reduced to per-task-type death-risk prediction, bypassing step-level observation entirely---a direction we leave for future work.
12	
13	\paragraph{Modality heterogeneity.}
14	The deliberate heterogeneity in deterministic modality definition---ground-truth state observation in ScienceWorld, code execution in MATH, admissible-action filtering in ALFWorld---tests whether the framework (episode-level counterfactual analysis) generalizes, not whether any single modality definition does. The consistent finding across structurally different modalities strengthens rather than weakens the conclusion that catastrophic-failure avoidance is the dominant routing signal wherever it exists.
15	
```

</details>

A lightweight death predictor may capture most routing benefit without full counterfactual labeling. In principle, routing could be fully reduced to per-task-type death-risk prediction, bypassing step-level observation entirely---a direction we leave for future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Modality heterogeneity.}\nThe deliberate heterogeneity in deterministic modality definition---ground-truth state observation in ScienceWorld, code execution in MATH, admissible-action filtering in ALFWorld---tests whether the framework (episode-level counterfactual analysis) generalizes, not whether any single modality definition does. The consistent finding across structurally different modalities strengthens rather than weakens the conclusion that catastrophic-failure avoidance is the dominant routing signal wherever it exists.\n",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 15
    }

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet understanding \emph{what} drives the value of routing decisions remains unclear. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to decompose routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Among task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) are driven by death asymmetry---a penalty-independent metric. Under the default penalty $P{=}100$, the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) is $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$), though DAF varies from 50\% ($P{=}10$) to 98\% ($P{=}500$) across penalty settings (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Per-task death rate correlates strongly with routing signal ($r{=}0.97$, though partly structural as death penalties contribute directly to score differences; \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}). An exploratory step-level router trained on these labels achieves $54.1 \pm 5.4\%$ step-level balanced accuracy (with same-episode leakage) but only $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes (below the always-deterministic baseline of 50\%); data scaling ($32{\times}$) confirms this at the 1.7B scale; scaling to 4B yields marginal episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) but remains far from practical. Cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$) shows the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable without a death penalty: the label distribution inverts from 86:9 (deterministic-preferred) to 30:17 (internal directionally favored), with a directional trend favoring internal reasoning ($+4.3$ percentage points, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$). A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, 70 episodes) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$ for Qwen) and zero internal-preferred episodes. These findings indicate that in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety---the routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection rather than efficiency optimization.
3	\end{abstract}
```

```text
29	In the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage is not statistically detectable ($p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$).
30	Second, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}), confirming the phenomenon generalizes across model families.
31	An exploratory step-level router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) trained on these counterfactual labels achieves $54.1 \pm 5.4\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but only $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes---below the always-deterministic baseline---a limitation at the 1.7B scale confirmed by data scaling ($32{\times}$) ablations; the 4B scale yields marginal episode-level improvement but remains impractical (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
32	
33	Our contributions are:
34	\begin{enumerate}
35	    \item \textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}
36	    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}).
37	
38	    \item \textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}
39	    On ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry. The penalty-dependent DAF metric reaches $90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ but varies substantially across penalty settings. An exploratory router trained on these labels fails to outperform the trivial always-deterministic baseline on held-out episodes ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ vs.\ $50.0\%$); data scaling ablations confirm this at the 1.7B scale; scaling to 4B yields marginal improvement ($59.0\%$) but remains far from practical when DAF $= 90.4\%$ and always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
40	
41	    \item \textbf{Cross-context validation.}
42	    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where no death penalty exists, internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpretation ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$), consistent with the absence of death-driven routing signal. On ALFWorld ($N{=}135$), deterministic success (27.4\%) far exceeds internal (3.0\%), but the mechanism is information asymmetry (access to admissible actions) rather than death avoidance (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Both contrasts confirm that the ScienceWorld finding is driven by failure structure, not an inherent advantage of deterministic computation.
43	\end{enumerate}
```

_source line 892 · 2026-05-16T13:09:13.045Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Xy6wAfZvXnz33qKBLUggfh`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We introduced episode-level counterfactual analysis for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes (11 of 30 task types, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry. The DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$) at $P{=}100$, with strong cross-task correlation ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$).
4	
5	Cross-environment evidence supports this finding: on MATH ($N{=}300$), the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable without death penalty ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$). A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, 70 episodes) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks with a larger differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$). An exploratory router trained on counterfactual labels achieves only $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes---below always-deterministic---a limitation at the 1.7B scale confirmed by data scaling; capacity scaling to 4B yields marginal episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) but remains far from practical.
6	
7	In environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety: the routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection rather than efficiency optimization. Open directions include step-level counterfactual labeling, stronger backbones with lower intrinsic death rates, and environments with graded failure costs.
8	
```

```text
122	
123	\paragraph{Evaluation methods.} All results use \emph{generation-based evaluation}: the model generates a routing label as text (\texttt{enable\_thinking=False}), parsed for classification. We evaluate at two granularities: \emph{step-level} (steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets) and \emph{episode-level} (entire episodes held out). Step-level accuracy ($54.1 \pm 5.4\%$) exceeds chance, indicating the router captures some signal, but episode-level accuracy ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below chance) shows this does not generalize to unseen episodes. A data scaling ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}) rules out step-level data scarcity, pointing instead to a structural limitation: always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal given DAF $= 90.4\%$. Both metrics are reported in the main text; the model-size ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}) uses step-level evaluation.
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 921 · 2026-05-16T13:10:26.842Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NQFL9dKZmc8dkJS7KdKNJ4`</summary>

```text
215	
216	All scales hover at ${\sim}50\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy---equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline---with no monotonic improvement as data increases. This rules out step-level data scarcity as the explanation for the router's episode-level failure; episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics---a $5.5{\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\%$ signal. The failure points instead to a structural limitation: when DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router to exploit.
```

</details>

     ```latex
     We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. A classification head on LLM hidden states degenerates to always-deterministic across all configurations. An implicit router (Qwen3-1.7B fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) yields $54.1 \pm 5.4\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes (8 seeds)---below always-deterministic (50\%). This reveals a capacity-dependent negative: at practical router scales (0.6B--1.7B), episode-level balanced accuracy remains at or below chance ($50.67\%$/$47.1\%$). Scaling to 4B yields $58.96 \pm 1.94\%$ ($p < 0.005$ vs.\ random; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations})---confirming that episode-level signal exists but is capacity-starved. Data scaling ($25\%$--$800\%$) shows no improvement at any capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). The cost of extracting this signal (4B parameters for $+9$\,pp above random) is disproportionate to the routing benefit, validating our analytical methodology as the efficient alternative to brute-force learned routing. Deployed end-to-end (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), the router ($-10.70$) does not outperform always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). We verify label quality in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
     ```

        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet understanding \\emph{what} drives the value of routing decisions remains unclear. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to decompose routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Among task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) are driven by death asymmetry---a penalty-independent metric. Under the default penalty $P{=}100$, the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) is $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), though DAF varies from 50\\% ($P{=}10$) to 98\\% ($P{=}500$) across penalty settings (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Per-task death rate correlates strongly with routing signal ($r{=}0.97$, though partly structural as death penalties contribute directly to score differences; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). An exploratory step-level router trained on these labels achieves $54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$ step-level balanced accuracy (with same-episode leakage) but only $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ on held-out episodes (below the always-deterministic baseline of 50\\%); data scaling ($32{\\times}$) confirms this capacity-dependent limitation; scaling to 4B yields $59.0\\%$ episode-level accuracy ($p < 0.005$ vs.\\ random) but the cost (4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp) is disproportionate to the routing benefit. Cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$) shows the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable without a death penalty: the label distribution inverts from 86:9 (deterministic-preferred) to 30:17 (internal directionally favored), with a directional trend favoring internal reasoning ($+4.3$ percentage points, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$). A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, 70 episodes) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen) and zero internal-preferred episodes. These findings indicate that in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety---the routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection rather than efficiency optimization.\n\\end{abstract}",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 3
      }

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe introduced episode-level counterfactual analysis for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes (11 of 30 task types, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry. The DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$) at $P{=}100$, with strong cross-task correlation ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$).\n\nCross-environment evidence supports this finding: on MATH ($N{=}300$), the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable without death penalty ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$). A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, 70 episodes) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks with a larger differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$). An exploratory router trained on counterfactual labels achieves only $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ on held-out episodes---below always-deterministic---a capacity-dependent limitation confirmed by data scaling; the 4B scale yields $59.0\\%$ episode-level accuracy but the cost is disproportionate to the routing benefit.\n\nIn environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety: the routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection rather than efficiency optimization. Open directions include step-level counterfactual labeling, stronger backbones with lower intrinsic death rates, and environments with graded failure costs.\n",
        "numLines": 8,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8
      }
    },

      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\n\nWe find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \\textbf{death avoidance accounts for 90.4\\% of the routing signal} (DAF $= 90.4\\%$, 95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$; at default penalty $P{=}100$) in ScienceWorld.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$.\n\nReaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nWe instead propose \\emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\n\nAmong task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types, 245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 78.9\\% of non-tie labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.\nThe penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \\S\\ref{sec:daf}) is $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$) at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 50\\% ($P{=}10$) to 98\\% ($P{=}500$).\nThe per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a directional trend consistent with the absence of death-driven routing signal: internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter by $+4.3$~pp ($p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$)---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.\nIn the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage is not statistically detectable ($p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$).\nSecond, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}), confirming the phenomenon generalizes across model families.\nAn exploratory step-level router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) trained on these counterfactual labels achieves $54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but only $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ on held-out episodes---below the always-deterministic baseline---a capacity-dependent limitation confirmed by data scaling ($32{\\times}$) ablations; the 4B scale yields $59.0\\%$ but the cost is disproportionate to the routing benefit (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}\n    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}\n    On ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry. The penalty-dependent DAF metric reaches $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ but varies substantially across penalty settings. An exploratory router trained on these labels fails to outperform the trivial always-deterministic baseline on held-out episodes ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ vs.\\ $50.0\\%$); data scaling ablations confirm this capacity-dependent limitation; scaling to 4B yields $59.0\\%$ episode-level accuracy but the cost (4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp above random) is disproportionate when DAF $= 90.4\\%$ and always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-context validation.}\n    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where no death penalty exists, internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpretation ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$), consistent with the absence of death-driven routing signal. On ALFWorld ($N{=}135$), deterministic success (27.4\\%) far exceeds internal (3.0\\%), but the mechanism is information asymmetry (access to admissible actions) rather than death avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Both contrasts confirm that the ScienceWorld finding is driven by failure structure, not an inherent advantage of deterministic computation.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",
        "numLines": 46,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 46
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross 245 episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$; at default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). The internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$). Per-task, death rate differential correlates strongly with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}; robustness diagnostics in Appendix~\\ref{app:correlation}). A high correlation is expected whenever the scaled death-rate differential ($P \\times \\Delta_d$) dominates between-task variance---formally, when $\\text{Var}(P \\cdot \\Delta_d) \\gg \\text{Var}(\\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$. At $P{=}100$, this condition holds for all but one task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, which has zero deaths). The sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}) confirms that $r$ drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$, where this dominance condition no longer holds. Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\nOne exception: \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} ($n{=}40$, zero deaths) has DAF $= 0\\%$ with $\\Delta = +9.58$ entirely from non-death score differences; all 10 remaining types exceed DAF $> 85\\%$. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \\texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, $\\Delta = 21.8$, 95\\% CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed; see \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---\\emph{oracle degeneracy} indicating the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.\n\n\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. A classification head on LLM hidden states degenerates to always-deterministic across all configurations. An implicit router (Qwen3-1.7B fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) yields $54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ on held-out episodes (8 seeds)---below always-deterministic (50\\%). This reveals a capacity-dependent negative: at practical router scales (0.6B--1.7B), episode-level balanced accuracy remains at or below chance ($50.67\\%$/$47.1\\%$). Scaling to 4B yields $58.96 \\pm 1.94\\%$ ($p < 0.005$ vs.\\ random; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations})---confirming that episode-level signal exists but is capacity-starved. Data scaling ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) shows no improvement at any capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). The cost of extracting this signal (4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp above random) is disproportionate to the routing benefit, validating our analytical methodology as the efficient alternative to brute-force learned routing. Deployed end-to-end (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}), the router ($-10.70$) does not outperform always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). We verify label quality in Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}.\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, with a larger differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\\ref{app:failure-modes}--\\ref{app:ablations}.\n",

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

38	\end{table}

40	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---\emph{oracle degeneracy} indicating the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
41	
42	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic}
43	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
44	
45	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. A classification head on LLM hidden states degenerates to always-deterministic across all configurations. An implicit router (Qwen3-1.7B fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) yields $54.1 \pm 5.4\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ on held-out episodes (8 seeds)---below always-deterministic (50\%). This reveals a capacity-dependent negative: at practical router scales (0.6B--1.7B), episode-level balanced accuracy remains at or below chance ($50.67\%$/$47.1\%$). Scaling to 4B yields $58.96 \pm 1.94\%$ ($p < 0.005$ vs.\ random; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations})---confirming that episode-level signal exists but is capacity-starved. Data scaling ($25\%$--$800\%$) shows no improvement at any capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). The cost of extracting this signal (4B parameters for $+9$\,pp above random) is disproportionate to the routing benefit, validating our analytical methodology as the efficient alternative to brute-force learned routing. Deployed end-to-end (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), the router ($-10.70$) does not outperform always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). We verify label quality in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
46	
47	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, with a larger differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\ref{app:failure-modes}--\ref{app:ablations}.

_source line 989 · 2026-05-16T13:14:43.571Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The `review` option sets `\acl@linenumberstrue`. There's no `nolinenumbers` option defined. The simplest fix is to either:

1. Change `[review]` to `[preprint]` — but that disables anonymization which we might need
2. Add `\let\linenumbers\relax` after loading acl
3. After loading `acl.sty`, add `\acl@linenumbersfalse` — but that won't work because lineno is already loaded conditionally at package load time

Actually, looking at the acl.sty code more carefully:


The `\ifacl@linenumbers` conditional controls whether lineno gets loaded and the `\linenumbers` command gets set up during package initialization. The `\AtBeginDocument{\linenumbers}` hook is already registered at load time, so trying to disable it afterward won't work. Since `\usepackage[review]{acl}` triggers all of this during package loading, the cleanest approach is to redefine `\linenumbers` itself to do nothing. The director suggested prioritizing removing line numbers unless the template explicitly requires them. For ACL/EMNLP ARR submissions, the review option does include line numbers, but if they're overlapping with text, that's a problem we should solve. The cleanest approach is to load the acl package with review mode for anonymization, then disable line numbers by adding `\nolinenumbers` right after the document begins, since the lineno package will be available and that command will work. Looking at the document structure to find where `\begin{document}` is located so I can insert the `\nolinenumbers` command right after it.
```

  "old_text": "Data scaling ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) shows no improvement at any capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). ```text
33	
34	\section*{Limitations}
35	
36	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:discussion}, Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.
37	
38	\section*{Ethics Statement}
39	
40	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two consumer GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
41	
42	\bibliography{references}
43	
44	\appendix
45	
46	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
47	\label{app:labeling}
48	
49	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
50	
51	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
52	\label{app:per-task}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QJ9cnB5rrp1bK49QvTMDUs`</summary>

```text
270	
271	This sharpens our central finding: death is not merely the dominant component of routing signal---it is effectively the \emph{only} component that generates differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ reduces to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\Delta_{\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\text{DAF} = \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.
272	
273	\section{Cross-Model Verification}
274	\label{app:cross-model}
275	
276	To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\%) are deterministic-preferred, \textbf{0} (0\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\% of episodes versus 24.3\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$), compared to $+26.6\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.
277	
278	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
279	\label{app:failure-modes}
280	
281	The most common failure mode for the internal strategy is state hallucination: the model predicts that an action succeeded (e.g., ``the water is now boiling'') when the environment state did not change, leading to cascading errors that trigger agent death. The deterministic strategy avoids this by receiving ground-truth observations, but still dies in 16.3\% of episodes---primarily from selecting inherently dangerous actions (e.g., eating toxic substances) rather than from state misestimation.
282	
283	\section{Ablation Studies}
284	\label{app:ablations}
285	
286	\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\ref{tab:capacity}).
287	
288	\begin{table}[h]
289	\centering
290	\small
291	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
292	\toprule
293	Model & Step Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) & Ep.\ Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) \\
294	\midrule
295	Qwen3-0.6B & 52.8 & $50.67 \pm 2.62$ \\
296	Qwen3-1.7B\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} & 54.1 & $47.10 \pm 2.20$ \\
297	Qwen3-4B & 53.5 & $\mathbf{58.96 \pm 1.94}$ \\
298	\midrule
299	Always-det & 50.0 & 50.0 \\
300	\bottomrule
301	\end{tabular}
302	\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy. Step-level accuracy is flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\%$). At episode level, 4B first breaks the random baseline ($58.96\%$, 95\% CI $[55.1\%, 62.8\%]$). $\dagger$\,1.7B episode-level figure comes from the main experiment (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) with a different training configuration (8 cross-split seeds vs.\ 4 seeds for 0.6B/4B); direct comparison across scales should be interpreted with this caveat.}
303	\label{tab:capacity}
304	\end{table}
305	
306	Step-level balanced accuracy is essentially flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\%$), indicating that step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, however, the 4B model first breaks the random baseline ($58.96 \pm 1.94\%$, 95\% CI $[55.1\%, 62.8\%]$), suggesting that model capacity is one limiting factor for episode-level generalization. Nevertheless, even 4B achieves only ${\sim}59\%$---far from a practically useful router---and the dominant explanation remains that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently.
307	
308	\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\% vs.\ 42.5\%), confirming that reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.
309	
310	\section{Cross-Environment Analysis: ALFWorld}
311	\label{app:cross-env}
312	
313	We evaluate on ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment with 6 task types (json\_2.1.1 split, valid\_seen, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric), using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty---failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step. This is an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch.
314	
315	The success rate gap is large: 27.4\% (deterministic) vs.\ 3.0\% (internal), reflecting the 7B model's near-inability to solve household tasks without admissible-action guidance. The label distribution (Table~\ref{tab:cross-env}) shows 35 deterministic-preferred (25.9\%), 2 internal-preferred (1.5\%), and 98 no-preference (72.6\%), of which 96 are both-fail. The deterministic advantage stems entirely from the information gap rather than death avoidance.
316	
317	\begin{figure}[h]
318	\centering
319	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}
5	Does routing signal persist without catastrophic failure? On MATH Level~4--5 (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, internal reasoning directionally outperforms: 81.0\% vs.\ 76.7\% accuracy ($+4.3$~pp, 95\% CI $[0.0, +8.7]$; McNemar $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$). The label distribution inverts: 30 internal-preferred vs.\ 17 deterministic-preferred (cf.\ 9:86 in ScienceWorld), with 84.3\% no-preference. In the absence of death risk, the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable---consistent with death avoidance as the primary driver. A complementary evaluation on ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) confirms that the counterfactual framework identifies different routing mechanisms across environments: the deterministic advantage there stems from information asymmetry (access to admissible actions) rather than death avoidance (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Penalty sensitivity.}
8	The $r = 0.97$ correlation is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$); defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$). The correlation emerges only at $P \geq 50$ ($r = 0.86$) and reflects that non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Death is effectively the \emph{only} component generating differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. These findings may be capability-dependent: the 42.9\% internal death rate reflects a 7B model's hallucination vulnerability; larger models with lower death rates would reduce DAF, potentially revealing non-death routing signal currently masked by catastrophic failures.
9	
10	\paragraph{Implications for router design.}
11	The dominance of death avoidance (DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$) reframes modality routing as primarily a \emph{safety} problem rather than an efficiency-quality tradeoff. The routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection---closer to a conservative default policy than to a learned safety critic---aligning more closely with safe RL~\citep{garcia2015comprehensive} than with cost-quality optimization~\citep{cogrouter-2026, ares-2026}. The extreme label imbalance (86:9 deterministic-preferred, 150 no-preference) is not an artifact but a central result: among episodes where modality matters, deterministic is preferred at 9.6:1, explaining why always-deterministic is a strong baseline and why the trained router (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) adds no value. A lightweight death predictor may capture most routing benefit without full counterfactual labeling. The capacity scaling results reinforce this: extracting episode-level routing signal requires 4B parameters for only $+9$\,pp above random ($59.0\%$), a disproportionate cost that validates our counterfactual analytical methodology as the efficient alternative to brute-force learned routing. In principle, routing could be fully reduced to per-task-type death-risk prediction, bypassing step-level observation entirely---a direction we leave for future work.
12	
13	\paragraph{Modality heterogeneity.}
14	The deliberate heterogeneity in deterministic modality definition---ground-truth state observation in ScienceWorld, code execution in MATH, admissible-action filtering in ALFWorld---tests whether the framework (episode-level counterfactual analysis) generalizes, not whether any single modality definition does. The consistent finding across structurally different modalities strengthens rather than weakens the conclusion that catastrophic-failure avoidance is the dominant routing signal wherever it exists.
15	
```

```text
33	Our contributions are:
34	\begin{enumerate}
35	    \item \textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}
36	    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}).
37	
38	    \item \textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}
39	    On ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry. The penalty-dependent DAF metric reaches $90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ but varies substantially across penalty settings. An exploratory router trained on these labels fails to outperform the trivial always-deterministic baseline on held-out episodes ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ vs.\ $50.0\%$); data scaling ablations confirm this capacity-dependent limitation; scaling to 4B yields $59.0\%$ episode-level accuracy but the cost (4B parameters for $+9$\,pp above random) is disproportionate when DAF $= 90.4\%$ and always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
40	
41	    \item \textbf{Cross-context validation.}
42	    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where no death penalty exists, internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpretation ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\alpha{=}0.05$), consistent with the absence of death-driven routing signal. On ALFWorld ($N{=}135$), deterministic success (27.4\%) far exceeds internal (3.0\%), but the mechanism is information asymmetry (access to admissible actions) rather than death avoidance (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Both contrasts confirm that the ScienceWorld finding is driven by failure structure, not an inherent advantage of deterministic computation.
43	\end{enumerate}
44	
45	
46	
```
